</p><p>The exposure of Kiribati to changes in sea levels is exacerbated by the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_decadal_oscillation" title="Pacific decadal oscillation">Pacific decadal oscillation</a>, which is a climate switch phenomenon that results in changes from periods of <a href="/wiki/La_Ni%C3%B1a" title="La Niña">La Niña</a> to periods of <a href="/wiki/El_Ni%C3%B1o" title="El Niño">El Niño</a>. This has an effect on sea levels. For example, in 2000, there was a switch from periods of downward pressure of El Niño on sea levels to an upward pressure of La Niña on sea levels, which upward pressure causes more frequent and higher high tide levels. The <a href="/wiki/Perigean_spring_tide" title="Perigean spring tide">Perigean spring tide</a> (often called a <a href="/wiki/King_tide" title="King tide">king tide</a>) can result in seawater flooding low-lying areas of the islands of Kiribati.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Onotoa.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Onotoa.png/220px-Onotoa.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Onotoa.png/330px-Onotoa.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Onotoa.png/440px-Onotoa.png 2x" data-file-width="3648" data-file-height="2736" /></a><figcaption>South of <a href="/wiki/Onotoa" title="Onotoa">Onotoa</a> Atoll</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg/220px-South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="115" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg/330px-South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg/440px-South_Tarawa_from_the_air.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1569" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Tarawa_Atoll" class="mw-redirect" title="Tarawa Atoll">Tarawa Atoll</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Atolls" class="mw-redirect" title="Atolls">atolls</a> and <a href="/wiki/Reef" title="Reef">reef</a> islands can respond to changes in sea-level. Paul Kench at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Auckland" title="University of Auckland">University of Auckland</a> in New Zealand and Arthur Webb at the <a href="/wiki/South_Pacific_Applied_Geoscience_Commission" title="South Pacific Applied Geoscience Commission">South Pacific Applied Geoscience Commission</a> in Fiji released a study in 2010 on the dynamic response of atolls and reef islands in the central Pacific. Kiribati was mentioned in the study, and Webb and Kench found that the three major urbanised islands in Kiribati—Betio, Bairiki and Nanikai—increased by 30% (36 hectares), 16.3% (5.8 hectares) and 12.5% (0.8 hectares), respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-WZ_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WZ-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Elsevier_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Elsevier-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-PSK2014_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PSK2014-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-AWPK_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AWPK-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-NG15_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NG15-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The study by Paul Kench and Arthur Webb recognises that the islands are extremely vulnerable to sea level rise, and concluded that: "This study did not measure vertical growth of the island surface nor does it suggest there is any change in the height of the islands. Since land height has not changed the vulnerability of the greater part of the land area of each island to submergence due to sea level rise is also unchanged and these low-lying atolls remain immediately and extremely vulnerable to inundation or sea water flooding."<sup id="cite_ref-Elsevier_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Elsevier-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Climate Change in the Pacific Report of 2011 describes Kiribati as having a low risk of <a href="/wiki/Cyclones" class="mw-redirect" title="Cyclones">cyclones</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> however in March 2015 Kiribati experienced flooding and destruction of seawalls and coastal infrastructure as the result of <a href="/wiki/Cyclone_Pam" title="Cyclone Pam">Cyclone Pam</a>, a Category 5 cyclone that devastated <a href="/wiki/Vanuatu" title="Vanuatu">Vanuatu</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SBS15_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SBS15-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Kiribati remains exposed to the risk that cyclones can strip the low-lying islands of their vegetation and soil.
</p><p>Gradual sea-level rise also allows for coral <a href="/wiki/Polyp_(zoology)" title="Polyp (zoology)">polyp</a> activity to raise the atolls with the sea level. However, if the increase in sea level occurs at a rate faster than coral growth, or if polyp activity is damaged by <a href="/wiki/Ocean_acidification" title="Ocean acidification">ocean acidification</a>, then the resilience of the atolls and reef islands is less certain.<sup id="cite_ref-PK14_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PK14-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Also, <a href="/wiki/Coral_bleaching" title="Coral bleaching">coral bleaching</a> has occurred on more than 60% of the coral reefs in the Maldives.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Human Rights Measurement Initiative<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> finds that the climate crisis has worsened human rights conditions moderately (4.8 out of 6) in Kiribati.<sup id="cite_ref-Kiribati_-_HRMI_Rights_Tracker_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kiribati_-_HRMI_Rights_Tracker-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Human rights experts reported that the climate crisis has compromised access to food and clean water, as well as women's rights, housing security and cultural integrity.<sup id="cite_ref-Kiribati_-_HRMI_Rights_Tracker_103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kiribati_-_HRMI_Rights_Tracker-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Kiribati_Adaptation_Program" title="Kiribati Adaptation Program">Kiribati Adaptation Program</a> (KAP), started in 2003, is a US$5.5&#160;million initiative that was originally enacted by the national government of Kiribati with the support of the <a href="/wiki/Global_Environment_Facility" title="Global Environment Facility">Global Environment Facility</a> (GEF), the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>, the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="United Nations Development Program">United Nations Development Program</a>, and the Japanese government. Australia later joined the coalition, donating US$1.5&#160;million to the effort. The program aims to take place over six years, supporting measures that reduce Kiribati's vulnerability to the effects of <a href="/wiki/Climate_change" title="Climate change">climate change</a> and sea level rise by raising awareness of climate change, assessing and protecting available water resources, and managing inundation.
At the start of the Adaptation Program, representatives from each of the inhabited atolls identified key climatic changes that had taken place over the past 20–40 years and proposed coping mechanisms to deal with these changes under four categories of urgency of need. The program is now focusing on the country's most vulnerable sectors in the most highly populated areas.
Initiatives include improving water supply management in and around Tarawa; <a href="/wiki/Coastal_management" title="Coastal management">coastal management</a> protection measures such as mangrove re-plantation and protection of public infrastructure; strengthening laws to reduce coastal erosion; and population settlement planning to reduce personal risks.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government has taken specific action to ensure <a href="/wiki/Food_security" title="Food security">food security</a>, as sea level rise, drought, and overfishing have created food and water shortages. This has involved diversifying food sources and ensuring existing resources are managed sustainably.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The issue of <a href="/wiki/Plastic_pollution" title="Plastic pollution">plastic pollution</a> has also been a key challenge for Kiribati as it hurts both its marine biodiversity and its economy that relies  primarily on tourism and fishery.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> As a result, the government of Kiribati, more specifically the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Environment_and_Conservation_Division&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Environment and Conservation Division (page does not exist)">Environment and Conservation Division</a> (ECD) which forms part of the Kiribati Government's Ministry of Environment, Lands and Agricultural Development, has made efforts to tackle this issue nationally through environment acts, and state policy papers.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_106-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> To a further extent, it has also recognized the global nature of plastic pollution, and consequently, has promoted international cooperation and multilateral solutions.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> This is notably observable during the current negotiations of the <a href="/wiki/Global_plastic_pollution_treaty" title="Global plastic pollution treaty">Global Plastic Pollution Treaty</a> planned to be finally drafted by the end of 2024.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Climate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg/220px-Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg/330px-Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg/440px-Line5304_-_Flickr_-_NOAA_Photo_Library.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption>A tropical islet with palm fronds oriented in the direction of the prevailing winds.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kiribati has a <a href="/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate" title="Tropical rainforest climate">tropical rainforest climate</a> (<a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification" title="Köppen climate classification">Af</a>). From April to October, there are predominant northeastern winds and stable temperatures close to 30&#160;°C (86&#160;°F). From November to April, western gales bring rain.
</p><p>Kiribati wet season (<i>te Auu-Meang</i>) also recognized as the <a href="/wiki/Tropical_cyclone" title="Tropical cyclone">Tropical cyclone</a> (TC) (<i>te Angibuaka</i>) season starts from November to April every year. Kiribati therefore typically experiences more extreme weather events associated with <a href="/wiki/Tropical_disturbance" class="mw-redirect" title="Tropical disturbance">Tropical disturbances</a> (TD) or Tropical cyclones during te Auu-Meang. But Tropical cyclones rarely develop or pass along the equator where Kiribati is located. However, based on past events, Kiribati has been impacted from distant Tropical cyclone (TC) and the impacts were observed while the systems are in their development stages (Tropical Low/disturbance) or even before they reach Tropical cyclone category.
</p><p>The fair season starts when <i>Ten Rimwimata</i> (<a href="/wiki/Antares" title="Antares">Antares</a>) appears in the sky after sunset, from May to November, when more gentle winds and currents and less rain. Then towards December, when <i>Nei Auti</i> (<a href="/wiki/Pleiades" title="Pleiades">Pleiades</a>) replaces Antares, the season of sudden westerly winds and more heavy rain discourages any far travel from island to island.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kiribati does not experience cyclones but effects may occasionally be experienced during cyclone seasons affecting nearby Pacific Island countries such as Fiji.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-brit_29-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brit-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas20033_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas20033-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Precipitation varies significantly between islands. For example, the annual average is 3,000&#160;mm (120&#160;in) in the north and 500&#160;mm (20&#160;in) in the south of the Gilbert Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-brit_29-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brit-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Most of these islands are in the dry belt of the equatorial oceanic climatic zone and experience prolonged droughts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas20033_88-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas20033-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecology">Ecology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Ecology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Kiribati" title="Wildlife of Kiribati">Wildlife of Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg/220px-AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="374" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg/330px-AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg/440px-AcrocephalusPistorKeulemans.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1915" data-file-height="3253" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Bokikokiko" title="Bokikokiko">bokikokiko</a> (<i>Acrocephalus aequinoctialis</i>) is the only land wildlife species endemic to Kiribati.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kiribati contains three ecosystems: <a href="/wiki/Central_Polynesian_tropical_moist_forests" title="Central Polynesian tropical moist forests">Central Polynesian tropical moist forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Micronesia_tropical_moist_forests" title="Eastern Micronesia tropical moist forests">Eastern Micronesia tropical moist forests</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Western_Polynesian_tropical_moist_forests" title="Western Polynesian tropical moist forests">Western Polynesian tropical moist forests</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Because of the relatively young geological age of the islands and atolls and high level of <a href="/wiki/Soil_salination" class="mw-redirect" title="Soil salination">soil salination</a>, the flora of Kiribati is somewhat unhealthy. The Gilbert Islands contain about 83 indigenous and 306 introduced plants, whereas the corresponding numbers for Line and Phoenix Islands are 67 and 283. None of these species are <a href="/wiki/Endemism" title="Endemism">endemic</a>, and about half of the indigenous ones have a limited distribution and became endangered or nearly extinct due to human activities such as phosphate mining.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200322_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200322-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Coconut" title="Coconut">Coconut</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pandanus_tectorius" title="Pandanus tectorius">pandanus</a> palms and <a href="/wiki/Breadfruit" title="Breadfruit">breadfruit</a> trees are the most common <i>wild</i> plants,<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-brit_29-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brit-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> whereas the five most cultivated crops but the traditional <i>Babai</i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Cyrtosperma_merkusii" title="Cyrtosperma merkusii">Cyrtosperma merkusii</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> are imported <a href="/wiki/Chinese_cabbage" title="Chinese cabbage">Chinese cabbage</a>, pumpkin, tomato, watermelon and cucumber.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200314_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200314-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Over eighty per cent of the population participates in either farming or fishing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMoseley2014191_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoseley2014191-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Seaweed_farming" title="Seaweed farming">Seaweed farming</a> is an important part of the economy<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The reason for this is unclear. (February 2017)">why?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>, with two major species <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Eucheuma_alcarezii&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Eucheuma alcarezii (page does not exist)">Eucheuma alcarezii</a></i> and <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Eucheuma_spinosium&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Eucheuma spinosium (page does not exist)">Eucheuma spinosium</a></i> introduced to the local lagoons from the Philippines in 1977. It competes with collection of the black-lipped pearl oyster (<i><a href="/wiki/Pinctada_margaritifera" title="Pinctada margaritifera">Pinctada margaritifera</a></i>) and shellfish,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200317_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200317-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> which are dominated by the strombid gastropod (<i><a href="/wiki/Strombus_luhuanus" class="mw-redirect" title="Strombus luhuanus">Strombus luhuanus</a></i>) and Anadara cockles (<i><a href="/wiki/Anadara" title="Anadara">Anadara</a> uropigimelana</i>), whereas the stocks of the giant clam (<i><a href="/wiki/Tridacna_gigas" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridacna gigas">Tridacna gigas</a></i>) have been largely exhausted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200317–19_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200317–19-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kiribati has a few land mammals, none being indigenous or endemic. They include the Polynesian rat (<i><a href="/wiki/Rattus_exulans" class="mw-redirect" title="Rattus exulans">Rattus exulans</a></i>), dogs, cats and pigs. Among the 75 bird species, the <a href="/wiki/Bokikokiko" title="Bokikokiko">Bokikokiko</a> (<i>Acrocephalus aequinoctialis</i>) is endemic to <a href="/wiki/Kiritimati" title="Kiritimati">Kiritimati</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200322_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200322-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are 600–800 species of inshore and pelagic finfish, some 200 species of corals and about 1000 species of shellfish.<sup id="cite_ref-LPS_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LPS-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200323_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200323-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Fishing mostly targets the family <a href="/wiki/Scombridae" title="Scombridae">Scombridae</a>, particularly the <a href="/wiki/Skipjack_tuna" title="Skipjack tuna">skipjack tuna</a> and <a href="/wiki/Yellowfin_tuna" title="Yellowfin tuna">yellowfin tuna</a> as well as <a href="/wiki/Flying_fish" title="Flying fish">flying fish</a> (<i>Cypselurus</i> spp.).
<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas200315_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200315-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dogs were already accompanying the first inhabitants but were re-introduced by European settlers: they have continued to grow in numbers and are roaming in traditional packs,<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> particularly around South Tarawa.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Kiribati" title="Economy of Kiribati">Economy of Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kiribatisupermarket.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Kiribatisupermarket.jpg/220px-Kiribatisupermarket.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Kiribatisupermarket.jpg/330px-Kiribatisupermarket.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Kiribatisupermarket.jpg/440px-Kiribatisupermarket.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="450" /></a><figcaption>A Bosj's warehouse in Kiribati.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kiribati_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Kiribati_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/220px-Kiribati_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Kiribati_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/330px-Kiribati_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Kiribati_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/440px-Kiribati_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a><figcaption>A proportional representation of Kiribati exports, 2019</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kiribati has few natural resources. Commercially viable <a href="/wiki/Phosphate" title="Phosphate">phosphate</a> deposits on <a href="/wiki/Banaba" title="Banaba">Banaba</a> were exhausted at the time of independence. <a href="/wiki/Copra" title="Copra">Copra</a> and fish now represent the bulk of production and exports. Kiribati has the lowest GDP out of any sovereign state in <a href="/wiki/Oceania" title="Oceania">Oceania</a>, and is considered one of the <a href="/wiki/Least_developed_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Least developed country">least developed countries</a> in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In one form or another, Kiribati gets a large portion of its income from abroad. Examples include fishing licences, development assistance, workers' <a href="/wiki/Remittance" title="Remittance">remittances</a>, especially the seafarers issued from <a href="/wiki/Marine_Training_Centre" title="Marine Training Centre">Marine Training Centre</a>, and a few tourists. Given Kiribati's limited domestic production ability, it must import nearly all of its essential foodstuffs and manufactured items; it depends on these external sources of income for financing.
</p><p>The economy of Kiribati benefits from international development assistance programs. The multilateral donors providing development assistance in 2009 were the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> (A$9&#160;million), the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme" title="United Nations Development Programme">United Nations Development Programme</a> (A$3.7&#160;million), UNICEF, and the <a href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization" title="World Health Organization">World Health Organization</a> (A$100,000).<sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_SA_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011_SA-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The bilateral donors providing development assistance in 2009 were Australia (A$11&#160;million), Japan (A$2&#160;million), New Zealand (A$6.6&#160;million), <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a> (A$10.6&#160;million), and other donors providing A$16.2&#160;million, including technical assistance grants from the <a href="/wiki/Asian_Development_Bank" title="Asian Development Bank">Asian Development Bank</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_SA_124-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011_SA-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The major donors in 2010/2011 were Australia (A$15&#160;million), Taiwan (A$11&#160;million); New Zealand (A$6&#160;million), the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> (A$4&#160;million) and the <a href="/wiki/Asian_Development_Bank" title="Asian Development Bank">Asian Development Bank</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-mfat_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mfat-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1956, <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_and_Ellice_Islands" title="Gilbert and Ellice Islands">Gilbert and Ellice Islands</a> established a <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_wealth_fund" title="Sovereign wealth fund">sovereign wealth fund</a> to act as a store of wealth for the country's earnings from <a href="/wiki/Phosphate" title="Phosphate">phosphate</a> mining.
In 2008, the <a href="/wiki/Revenue_Equalization_Reserve_Fund" title="Revenue Equalization Reserve Fund">Revenue Equalization Reserve Fund</a> was valued at US$400&#160;million.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> The RERF assets declined from A$637&#160;million (420% of GDP) in 2007 to A$570.5&#160;million (350% of GDP) in 2009<sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_SA_124-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011_SA-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> as the result of the <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%9308" class="mw-redirect" title="Financial crisis of 2007–08">global financial crisis</a> and exposure to failed Icelandic banks. In addition, draw-downs were made by the government of Kiribati to finance budgetary shortfalls during this period.<sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 2011, the IMF country report assessment of the economy of Kiribati is that "After two years of contraction, the economy recovered in the second half of 2010 and inflation pressure dissipated. It is estimated to have grown by 1.75% for the year. Despite a weather-related drop in copra production, private sector activity appears to have picked up, especially in retail. Tourist arrivals rebounded by 20% compared to 2009, although from a very low base.
Despite the rise in world food and fuel prices, inflation has bounced from 2008 crisis-highs into negative territory, reflecting the strong appreciation of the Australian dollar, which is used as the domestic currency, and a decline in the world price of rice. Credit growth in the overall economy declined in 2009 as economic activity stalled. But it started to pick up in the second half of 2010 as the recovery gained traction".<sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A major Australian bank, <a href="/wiki/Australia_and_New_Zealand_Banking_Group" class="mw-redirect" title="Australia and New Zealand Banking Group">ANZ</a>, maintains a presence on Kiribati<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> with a number of branches and <a href="/wiki/Automated_teller_machine" title="Automated teller machine">ATM</a> units.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ornamental_fish">Ornamental fish</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Ornamental fish">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Kiribati is a major exporter of hand-caught <a href="/wiki/Ornamental_fish" class="mw-redirect" title="Ornamental fish">ornamental fish</a>. There are eight licensed operators based on <a href="/wiki/Kiritimati" title="Kiritimati">Kiritimati</a> (Christmas Island). At the end of 2005, the number of pet fish exported was 110,000. All operators have a land-based facility but fish are kept in containers on the reef until the day before the shipment. This is to reduce the running cost and the mortality of pet fish to be exported. The flame angelfish (<i><a href="/wiki/Centropyge_loriculus" class="mw-redirect" title="Centropyge loriculus">Centropyge loriculus</a></i>) is the major species exported.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Transport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Kiribati" title="Transport in Kiribati">Transport in Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg/220px-Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg/330px-Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg/440px-Bonriki_International_Airport2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="450" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Bonriki_International_Airport" title="Bonriki International Airport">Bonriki International Airport</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kiribati has had two domestic airlines: <a href="/wiki/Air_Kiribati" title="Air Kiribati">Air Kiribati</a> and <a href="/wiki/Coral_Sun_Airways" title="Coral Sun Airways">Coral Sun Airways</a>. Both airlines are based in Tarawa's <a href="/wiki/Bonriki_International_Airport" title="Bonriki International Airport">Bonriki International Airport</a> and serve destinations across the Gilbert Islands and Line Islands only: <a href="/wiki/Banaba" title="Banaba">Banaba</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Phoenix_Islands" title="Phoenix Islands">Phoenix Islands</a> are not served by the domestic carriers.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Cassidy_International_Airport" title="Cassidy International Airport">Cassidy International Airport</a> on <a href="/wiki/Kiritimati" title="Kiritimati">Kiritimati</a> has an international service provided by <a href="/wiki/Fiji_Airways" title="Fiji Airways">Fiji Airways</a>: <a href="/wiki/Nadi_International_Airport" title="Nadi International Airport">Nadi</a> to Cassidy Airport and then to <a href="/wiki/Daniel_K._Inouye_International_Airport" title="Daniel K. Inouye International Airport">Honolulu</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Communications_and_media">Communications and media</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Communications and media">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The islands’ remote location in the Central Pacific at approximately the International Date Line and spanning hundreds of miles north and south of the equator has meant that communications between them has always been challenging and conducted primarily by radio and print media. <a href="/wiki/Television_Kiribati" title="Television Kiribati">TV Kiribati Ltd</a>, was owned by the government operated between 2004 and mid-2012, but could not reach all of the Islands. Radio Kiribati, based on Tarawa and operated by the government's Broadcasting and Publications Authority (BPA) on 1440&#160;kHz AM is the only form of mass media that reaches all the major islands. Transmission hours are limited and local content in Gilbertese is supplemented by English summaries and BBC News.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The BPA and a private broadcaster also operate FM stations accessible on Tarawa.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Savekiribati_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Savekiribati-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Inter-island communications for many years relied on a centralized shortwave radio network operated by Telecom Services Kiribati, Ltd (TSKL) based in each Island's Council Headquarters. Numerous issues including low availability, maintenance, privacy, and only one per island led TSKL to adopt satellite-based telephones. However, the system is more expensive and still only located at Council Headquarters.<sup id="cite_ref-Savekiribati_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Savekiribati-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Print weeklies in Gilbertese include the <i>Te Uekara</i> published by the government, <i>Te Mauri</i> published by the Kiribati Protestant Church, and the <i>Kiribati Independent</i>, published from <a href="/wiki/Auckland" title="Auckland">Auckland</a> as well as the <i>Kiribati Newstar</i>, published in English.<sup id="cite_ref-Savekiribati_132-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Savekiribati-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2019, SpaceX launched the <a href="/wiki/Kacific_Broadband_Satellites" title="Kacific Broadband Satellites">Kacific1 broadband satellite</a> that provides 100Mbit/s mobile and broadband service to 25 countries throughout to the Asia-Pacific region including Kiribati.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Three of the satellite's 56 spot beams provide overlapping coverage of the Gilbert Islands and Tuvalu; however, the more eastern regions of the country, the Phoenix and Line Islands, are outside of the satellite's coverage.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Southern Cross NEXT cable system, which entered service in July 2022,<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> connects the US to Australia and provides service to Kiribati (Tarawa) through the 234-mile (377&#160;km) Kiritimati Branch with one fiber pair. The network, which is an upgrade to the existing <a href="/wiki/Southern_Cross_Cable" title="Southern Cross Cable">Southern Cross Cable</a>, also connects to Samoa, Fiji, and New Zealand.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2021, the World Bank-backed procurement for the East Micronesian Cable system was cancelled due to security concerns. The undersea fiberoptic system, which would have originated in <a href="/wiki/Guam" title="Guam">Guam</a>, was "designed to improve the communications in the island nations of Nauru, Kiribati and Federated States of Micronesia (FSM)."<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> In January 2023, ministers from the three Pacific island nations signed a joint communiqué for moving forward with the stalled project. Funded by the U.S., Japan and Australia the project is valued at $70 million.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> In early 2023, it was reported that Kiribati became the first Pacific island nation to receive <a href="/wiki/Starlink" title="Starlink">Starlink</a> services.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Kiribati" title="Demographics of Kiribati">Demographics of Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Children_in_Bairiki_Square,_Tarawa,_Kiribati.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Children_in_Bairiki_Square%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.JPG/220px-Children_in_Bairiki_Square%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Children_in_Bairiki_Square%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.JPG/330px-Children_in_Bairiki_Square%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Children_in_Bairiki_Square%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.JPG/440px-Children_in_Bairiki_Square%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.JPG 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/I-Kiribati" class="mw-redirect" title="I-Kiribati">I-Kiribati</a> children in <a href="/wiki/South_Tarawa" title="South Tarawa">South Tarawa</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The November 2020 census showed a population of 119,940. About 90% lived in the <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Islands" title="Gilbert Islands">Gilbert Islands</a>, with 52.9% of them on South Tarawa, including <a href="/wiki/Betio" title="Betio">Betio</a>, the biggest township.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Until recently, people lived mostly in villages with populations between 50 and 3,000 on the outer islands. Most houses are made of materials obtained from coconut and pandanus trees. Frequent droughts and infertile soil hinder reliable large-scale agriculture, so the islanders have largely turned to the sea for livelihood and subsistence. Most are outrigger sailors and fishermen. Copra plantations serve as a second source of employment. In recent years, large numbers of citizens have moved to the more urban island capital of Tarawa, where Betio is the largest town and South Tarawa reunites larger towns like <a href="/wiki/Bikenibeu" title="Bikenibeu">Bikenibeu</a> or <a href="/wiki/Teaoraereke" title="Teaoraereke">Teaoraereke</a>. Increasing urbanisation has raised the population of South Tarawa to 63,017.<sup id="cite_ref-US_State_Dept_64-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_State_Dept-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_11-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (August 2017)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Landscape,_Kiribati,_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_(12425893525).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Landscape%2C_Kiribati%2C_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_%2812425893525%29.jpg/220px-Landscape%2C_Kiribati%2C_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_%2812425893525%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Landscape%2C_Kiribati%2C_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_%2812425893525%29.jpg/330px-Landscape%2C_Kiribati%2C_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_%2812425893525%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Landscape%2C_Kiribati%2C_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_%2812425893525%29.jpg/440px-Landscape%2C_Kiribati%2C_2011._Photo-_Erin_Magee_-_DFAT_%2812425893525%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2590" data-file-height="1726" /></a><figcaption>Village on Kiribati</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnicity">Ethnicity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Ethnicity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="barbox tright" style="overflow-x: auto;">
<div style="border:1px solid silver; font-size:88%; padding:0.4em; width:auto; background: white;">
<table style="text-align:left; border-collapse:collapse; width:100%;">

<td style="width:100px; text-align:left;"></td>

</tbody></table>
</div>
</div>
<p>The native people of Kiribati are called <a href="/wiki/I-Kiribati" class="mw-redirect" title="I-Kiribati">I-Kiribati</a>. Ethnically, the I-Kiribati are <a href="/wiki/Oceanic_languages" title="Oceanic languages">Oceanians</a>, a sub-ethnicity of <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_peoples" title="Austronesian peoples">Austronesians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Recent archaeological evidence indicates that Austronesians originally settled the islands thousands of years ago<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>. Around the 14th century, Fijians, Samoans, and Tongans invaded the islands, thus diversifying the ethnic range and introducing <a href="/wiki/Polynesian_languages" title="Polynesian languages">Polynesian</a> linguistic traits. Intermarriage among all ancestral groups, however, has led to a population reasonably homogeneous in appearance and traditions.<sup id="cite_ref-US_State_Dept_64-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_State_Dept-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The people of Kiribati speak <a href="/wiki/Gilbertese_language" title="Gilbertese language">Gilbertese</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Oceanic_languages" title="Oceanic languages">Oceanic language</a>. English is the other <a href="/wiki/Official_language" title="Official language">official language</a>, but is not used very often outside the island capital of <a href="/wiki/Tarawa" title="Tarawa">Tarawa</a>. It is more likely that some English words are mixed in their use with Gilbertese. Older generations of I-Kiribati tend to use more complicated versions of the language. Several words in Gilbertese have been adopted from European settlers, for instance, <i>kamea</i> is one of the Gilbertese words for dog, <i>kiri</i> being the Oceanic one,<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> which has its origins in the I-Kiribati people hearing the European settlers saying "come here" to their dogs, and adopting that as <i>kamea</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStearns1973228_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStearns1973228-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many other <a href="/wiki/Loanword" title="Loanword">loanwords</a> have been adopted (like <i>buun</i>, spoon, <i>moko</i>, smoke, <i>beeki</i>, pig, <i>batoro</i>, bottle) but some typical Gilbertese words are quite common, even for European objects (like <i>wanikiba</i>, plane – the flying canoe, <i>rebwerebwe</i>, motorbike – for the motor noise, <i>kauniwae</i>, shoes – the cow for the feet).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Kiribati" title="Religion in Kiribati">Religion in Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kiribati(008).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Kiribati%28008%29.JPG/220px-Kiribati%28008%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Kiribati%28008%29.JPG/330px-Kiribati%28008%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Kiribati%28008%29.JPG/440px-Kiribati%28008%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2816" data-file-height="2112" /></a><figcaption>Catholic Church in Betio</figcaption></figure>
<p>Christianity is the major religion in Kiribati, having been lately introduced by <a href="/wiki/Missionary" title="Missionary">missionaries</a>, because of its remoteness and the absence of any significant European presence until the latter half of the 19th century. The population is predominantly <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Kiribati" title="Catholic Church in Kiribati">Roman Catholic</a> (58.9%), with two main Protestant denominations (<a href="/wiki/Kiribati_Protestant_Church" title="Kiribati Protestant Church">Kiribati Protestant Church</a> 8.4% and <a href="/wiki/Kiribati_Uniting_Church" title="Kiribati Uniting Church">Kiribati Uniting Church</a> 21.2%) accounting for 29.6%. <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> (5.6%), <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith_in_Kiribati" title="Baháʼí Faith in Kiribati">Baháʼí Faith</a> (2.1%), <a href="/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist_Church" title="Seventh-day Adventist Church">Seventh-day Adventist Church</a> (2.1%), <a href="/wiki/Pentecostalism" title="Pentecostalism">Pentecostals</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses" title="Jehovah&#39;s Witnesses">Jehovah's Witnesses</a>, and other small faiths together account for less than 2% (2020 census).<sup id="cite_ref-Census_2020_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census_2020-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Health">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Oceania#Kiribati" class="mw-redirect" title="List of hospitals in Oceania">List of hospitals in Oceania §&#160;Kiribati</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Islands" title="Gilbert Islands">Gilbert Islands</a> where 90% of the Kiribati population live, boast some of the highest population densities in the Pacific, rivalling, without any tall building, cities like Hong Kong or Singapore. This overcrowding produces a great amount of pollution, worsening the quality and length of life. Due to insufficient sanitation and water filtration systems, worsened by the fragility of the <a href="/wiki/Lens_(hydrology)" title="Lens (hydrology)">water lens</a> of the atolls and by climate change, only about 66% have access to clean water. Waterborne diseases grow at record levels throughout the islands. Poor sanitation has led to an increase in cases of conjunctivitis, diarrhoea, dysentery, and fungal infections. Kiribati is the country with the third highest prevalence of smoking in the world, with 54–57% of the population reported as smokers.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Due to this and other "<a href="/wiki/Lifestyle_disease" title="Lifestyle disease">lifestyle diseases</a>," such as <a href="/wiki/Type_2_diabetes" title="Type 2 diabetes">type 2 diabetes</a>, there has been a drastic spike in amputations on the islands, doubling in a few years.
</p><p>As a consequence, the population of Kiribati has a quite low life expectancy at birth of 68.46 years.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Even if this data is of only 66.9 years, provided elsewhere, Kiribati ranks last in life expectancy out of the 20 nations of Oceania. This life expectancy is 64.3 for males, and 69.5 for females and there is an infant mortality rate of 41 deaths per 1,000 live births.<sup id="cite_ref-IMF_2011_11-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMF_2011-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">Tuberculosis</a> has a small presence in the country, with 365 cases per 100,000 a year.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Government expenditure on health was at US$268 per capita (PPP) in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> In 1990–2007, there were 23 physicians per 100,000 persons.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Since the arrival of Cuban doctors in 2006, the infant mortality rate has decreased significantly.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most health problems are related to consumption of semi-raw seafood, limited food storage facilities, and bacterial contamination of fresh water supplies. In the early 2000s, between 1 and 7% of the population, depending on the island, were annually treated for food poisoning in a hospital. Modernization and cross-cultural exchange of the late 20th century brought new issues of unhealthy diet and lifestyle, heavy smoking, especially among the young, and external infections, including HIV/AIDS.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas20038–9_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas20038–9-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Fresh water remains a concern of Kiribati – during the dry season (Aumaiaki), water has been drilled for instead of using rain water tanks. In recent years, there has been a longer than usual Aumaikai season resulting in additional water having to be drilled from beneath the water table. This has introduced water-borne illnesses, compounding the health problems within Kiribati.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Human Rights Measurement Initiative<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> finds that Kiribati is fulfilling 77.2% of what it should be fulfilling for the right to health based on its level of income.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> When looking at the right to health with respect to children, Kiribati achieves 93.8% of what is expected based on its current income.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_156-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> In regards to the right to health amongst the adult population, the country achieves 92.2% of what is expected based on the nation's level of income.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_156-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> Kiribati falls into the "very bad" category when evaluating the right to reproductive health because the nation is fulfilling only 45.5% of what the nation is expected to achieve based on the resources (income) it has available.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_156-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Kiribati" title="Education in Kiribati">Education in Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:University_of_South_Pacific,_Kiribati_Campus.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/University_of_South_Pacific%2C_Kiribati_Campus.jpg/220px-University_of_South_Pacific%2C_Kiribati_Campus.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/University_of_South_Pacific%2C_Kiribati_Campus.jpg/330px-University_of_South_Pacific%2C_Kiribati_Campus.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/University_of_South_Pacific%2C_Kiribati_Campus.jpg/440px-University_of_South_Pacific%2C_Kiribati_Campus.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2816" data-file-height="1584" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/University_of_the_South_Pacific" title="University of the South Pacific">University of the South Pacific</a>, Kiribati Campus</figcaption></figure>
<p>Primary education is free and compulsory for the first 9 years, beginning at age 6.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Mission schools are slowly being absorbed into the government primary school system. Higher education is expanding; students may seek technical, teacher or marine training, or study in other countries.<sup id="cite_ref-US_State_Dept_64-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_State_Dept-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Most choosing to do the latter have gone to Fiji to attend the <a href="/wiki/University_of_the_South_Pacific" title="University of the South Pacific">University of the South Pacific</a>, and those wishing to complete medical training have been sent to Australia, New Zealand or Cuba.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The education system is organised as follows:
</p>
<ul><li>Preschool for childhood from 1 to 5 years;</li>
<li>Primary school (Class 1 to 6) from 6 to 11 years;</li>
<li>Junior secondary school (Form 1 to 3) from 12 to 14;</li>
<li>Senior secondary school (Form 4 to 7) from 15 to 18.</li></ul>
<p><a href="/wiki/Kiribati_Ministry_of_Education" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiribati Ministry of Education">Kiribati Ministry of Education</a> is the education ministry. The government high schools are <a href="/wiki/King_George_V_and_Elaine_Bernacchi_School" title="King George V and Elaine Bernacchi School">King George V and Elaine Bernacchi School</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Tabiteuea_North_Senior_Secondary_School&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Tabiteuea North Senior Secondary School (page does not exist)">Tabiteuea North Senior Secondary School</a>, and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Melaengi_Tabai_Secondary_School&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Melaengi Tabai Secondary School (page does not exist)">Melaengi Tabai Secondary School</a>. 13 high schools are operated by Christian churches.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The University of the South Pacific has a campus in <a href="/wiki/Teaoraereke" title="Teaoraereke">Teaoraereke</a> for distant/flexible learning, but also to provide preparatory studies towards obtaining certificates, diplomas and degrees at other campus sites.
</p><p>The other prominent schools in Kiribati are:
</p>
<ul><li>the <a href="/wiki/Marine_Training_Centre" title="Marine Training Centre">Marine Training Centre</a> in Betio;</li>
<li>the Kiribati Institute of Technology;</li>
<li>the Kiribati Fisheries Training Centre;</li>
<li>the Kiribati School of Nursing;</li>
<li>the Kiribati Police Academy;</li>
<li>the Kiribati Teachers College.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Kiribati" title="Culture of Kiribati">Culture of Kiribati</a></div>
<p>Songs (<i>te anene</i>) and above all, dances (<i>te mwaie</i>), are held in high regard.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Kiribati" title="Music of Kiribati">Music of Kiribati</a></div>
<p>Kiribati folk music is generally based on <a href="/wiki/Chanting" class="mw-redirect" title="Chanting">chanting</a> or other forms of vocalising, accompanied by <a href="/wiki/Body_percussion" title="Body percussion">body percussion</a>. Public performances in modern Kiribati are generally performed by a seated chorus, accompanied by a guitar. However, during formal performances of the standing dance (<i>Te Kaimatoa</i>) or the hip dance (<i>Te Buki</i>), a wooden box is used as a percussion instrument. This box is constructed to give a hollow and reverberating tone when struck simultaneously by a chorus of men sitting around it. Traditional songs are often love-themed, but there are also competitive, religious, children's, patriotic, war and wedding songs. There are also <a href="/wiki/Tirere" class="mw-redirect" title="Tirere">stick dances</a> which accompany legends and semi-historical stories.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> These stick dances or "tirere" (pronounced seerere) are performed only during major festivals.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dance">Dance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Dance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Dance_in_Kiribati" title="Dance in Kiribati">Dance in Kiribati</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg/220px-Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg/330px-Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg/440px-Bonriki_International_Airport_welcome.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="450" /></a><figcaption>A welcome display.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The uniqueness of Kiribati when compared with other forms of Pacific island dance is its emphasis on the outstretched arms of the dancer and the sudden birdlike movement of the head. The <a href="/wiki/Great_frigatebird" title="Great frigatebird">Frigate bird</a> (<i>Fregata minor</i>) on the Kiribati flag refers to this bird-like style of Kiribati dancing. Most dances are in the standing or sitting position with movement limited and staggered. Smiling whilst dancing is generally considered vulgar within the context of Kiribati dancing. This is due to its origin of not being solely as a form of entertainment but as a form of storytelling and a display of the skill, beauty and endurance of the dancer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStevenson1896_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStevenson1896-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhincupWhincup2001_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhincupWhincup2001-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Cuisine">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Traditionally, the staple diet of the I-Kiribati was the abundance of seafood and coconuts. Starch based carbohydrate sources were not plentiful due to the hostile climate of the atolls with only the northernmost atolls being viable for constant agriculture. The national crop <i><a href="/wiki/Cyrtosperma_merkusii" title="Cyrtosperma merkusii">bwabwai</a></i> was only eaten during special celebrations along with pork. 
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Babai.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Babai.jpg/220px-Babai.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Babai.jpg/330px-Babai.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Babai.jpg/440px-Babai.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Bwabwai cultivation in <a href="/wiki/Butaritari" title="Butaritari">Butaritari</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>To complement the rather low consumption of carbohydrates in their diets, the I-Kiribati processed the sap and fruit of the abundant Pandanus and Coconut trees into different beverages and foods such as <i>te karewe</i> (fresh daily sap of the coconut tree) or <i><a href="/wiki/Te_tuae" class="mw-redirect" title="Te tuae">te tuae</a></i> (dried pandanus cake) and <i>te kabubu</i> (dried pandanus flour) from pandanus fruit pulp and <i>te kamaimai</i> (coconut sap syrup) from coconut sap.
</p><p>After World War II, rice became a daily staple in most households which is still the case today. Majority of seafood, fish in particular is eaten sashimi style with either coconut sap, soy sauce or vinegar based dressings in use often combined with chillies and onions.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Coconut_crabs" class="mw-redirect" title="Coconut crabs">Coconut crabs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mud_crabs" class="mw-redirect" title="Mud crabs">mud crabs</a> are traditionally given to breastfeeding mothers, with the belief that the meat stimulates the production of good quality <a href="/wiki/Breastmilk" class="mw-redirect" title="Breastmilk">breastmilk</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sport">Sport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Sport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Kiribati_at_the_Olympics" title="Kiribati at the Olympics">Kiribati at the Olympics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kiribati_at_the_2014_Commonwealth_Games" title="Kiribati at the 2014 Commonwealth Games">Kiribati at the 2014 Commonwealth Games</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bairiki_National_Stadium,_Tarawa,_Kiribati.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Bairiki_National_Stadium%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.jpg/220px-Bairiki_National_Stadium%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Bairiki_National_Stadium%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.jpg/330px-Bairiki_National_Stadium%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Bairiki_National_Stadium%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.jpg/440px-Bairiki_National_Stadium%2C_Tarawa%2C_Kiribati.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="1080" /></a><figcaption>Bairiki National Stadium, at Bairiki, Tarawa</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kiribati has competed at the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Games" title="Commonwealth Games">Commonwealth Games</a> since 1998 and the <a href="/wiki/Summer_Olympics" class="mw-redirect" title="Summer Olympics">Summer Olympics</a> since 2004. It sent three competitors to its first Olympics, two sprinters and a weightlifter.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> Kiribati won its first ever Commonwealth Games medal at the <a href="/wiki/2014_Commonwealth_Games" title="2014 Commonwealth Games">2014 Commonwealth Games</a> when weightlifter <a href="/wiki/David_Katoatau" title="David Katoatau">David Katoatau</a> won Gold in the 105&#160;kg Group.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">Football</a> is the most popular sport. <a href="/wiki/Kiribati_Islands_Football_Federation" title="Kiribati Islands Football Federation">Kiribati Islands Football Federation</a> (KIFF) is an associate member of the <a href="/wiki/Oceania_Football_Confederation" title="Oceania Football Confederation">Oceania Football Confederation</a>, but not of world-governing body <a href="/wiki/FIFA" title="FIFA">FIFA</a>. Instead, they are member of <a href="/wiki/ConIFA" class="mw-redirect" title="ConIFA">ConIFA</a>. <a href="/wiki/Kiribati_national_football_team" title="Kiribati national football team">Kiribati National team</a> has played ten matches, all of which it has lost, and all at the <a href="/wiki/Football_at_the_Pacific_Games" title="Football at the Pacific Games">Pacific Games</a> from 1979 to 2011. The Kiribati football stadium is <a href="/wiki/Bairiki_National_Stadium" title="Bairiki National Stadium">Bairiki National Stadium</a>, which has a capacity of 2,500.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/w/index.php?title=Betio_Soccer_Field&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Betio Soccer Field (page does not exist)">Betio Soccer Field</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://pl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betio_Soccer_Field" class="extiw" title="pl:Betio Soccer Field">pl</a>&#93;</span> is home to a number of local sporting teams.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Outside_perspectives">Outside perspectives</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kiribati&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Outside perspectives">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Edward_Carlyon_Eliot" title="Edward Carlyon Eliot">Edward Carlyon Eliot</a>, who was Resident Commissioner of the <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_and_Ellice_Islands" title="Gilbert and Ellice Islands">Gilbert and Ellice Islands</a> (now Kiribati &amp; Tuvalu) from 1913 to 1920 describes this period in his book <i>Broken Atoms</i> (autobiographical reminiscences) Pub. G. Bles, London, 1938.
</p><p>Sir <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Grimble" title="Arthur Grimble">Arthur Grimble</a> wrote about his time working in the British colonial service in Kiribati (then the Gilbert Islands) from 1914 to 1932 in two popular books <i>A Pattern of Islands</i> (1952)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrimble1952_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrimble1952-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> and <i>Return to the Islands</i> (1957). He also undertook academic studies of Gilbertese culture.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_Hilary_Smith" title="John Hilary Smith">John Smith</a>, the last governor of the <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_and_Ellice_Islands" title="Gilbert and Ellice Islands">Gilbert and Ellice Islands</a> wrote his memoir <i>An Island in the Autumn</i> (2011).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2011_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2011-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/J._Maarten_Troost" title="J. Maarten Troost">J. Maarten Troost</a>'s more recent autobiographical experiences in <a href="/wiki/Tarawa" title="Tarawa">Tarawa</a> are documented in his book <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sex_Lives_of_Cannibals" title="The Sex Lives of Cannibals">The Sex Lives of Cannibals</a></i> (2004).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETroost2004_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETroost2004-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alice Piciocchi's illustrated essay, <i>Kiribati. Cronache illustrate da una terra (s)perduta</i>, (2016) Milan: 24 ORE Cultura, also translated into French (2018, éditions du Rouergue), tries to write and portray a comprehensive encyclopaedic book of nowadays Kiribati.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-142"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-142">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20191029145745/http://www.mfed.gov.ki/statistics/documents/2015_Population_Census_Report_Volume_1final_211016.pdf">"2015 Population and Housing Census, Volume 1: Management Report and Basic Tables"</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. National Statistics Office (Ministry of Finance, Kiribati). September 2016. Archived from <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.mfed.gov.ki/statistics/documents/2015_Population_Census_Report_Volume_1final_211016.pdf">the original</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span> on 29 October 2019<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">17 August</span> 2017</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=2015+Population+and+Housing+Census%2C+Volume+1%3A+Management+Report+and+Basic+Tables&amp;rft.pub=National+Statistics+Office+%28Ministry+of+Finance%2C+Kiribati%29&amp;rft.date=2016-09&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.mfed.gov.ki%2Fstatistics%2Fdocuments%2F2015_Population_Census_Report_Volume_1final_211016.pdf&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AKiribati" class="Z3988"></span> The populations of Kiribati and of South Tarawa appear in Table 1b ("Population and No. of Households by Island, Ethnicity and Land Area: 2015") on page 32. The population of South Tarawa is 39,058. If the population of nearby <a href="/wiki/Betio" title="Betio">Betio</a> is included, the figure increases to 56,388. The population located in the Gilbert Islands is 99,633 and is given in Table A3 ("Population Summary by Island: 1931–2015") on page 195.</span>
